Worth knowing for any "should I unify these?" moment — when meaning differs, the right answer is usually two components with similar shape, not one with a sprawling prop set.

---

## chore/figma-fidelity-audit-6 (2026-05-07)

Sixth audit, after #50/#51/#52 shipped (map markers, safety rewire, en-route polish). Static sweep found zero design-system drift — recent PRs continue to include `chore:` self-review trim commits, so per-PR hygiene is doing the work. The substantive find was the en-route ETA wrapping to two rows on narrower devices and missing the sun/moon glyph that Figma uses in place of AM/PM.

- **Format the time manually when iOS's `toLocaleTimeString` won't drop AM/PM.** `toLocaleTimeString([], { hour: 'numeric', minute: '2-digit' })` honors locale for the AM/PM suffix even when you don't want it. There's no flag to suppress it cleanly across locales. Manual `${h12}:${minutes.padStart(2, '0')}` is more reliable when the design intentionally drops the suffix in favor of a glyph. Worth knowing for any "I want a specific time format and the locale API keeps adding things" moment.
- **A graphic can replace a text qualifier and free up a layout.** Figma uses a 16pt sun/moon glyph beside "8:30" instead of "8:30 PM" because the glyph (a) compactifies the layout (no wrap on narrow centerSlot widths) and (b) reads faster than three letters. Sun before 6am or after 6pm reads as "arriving in the dark"; sun otherwise. The 16pt left-spacer keeps the time optically centered against the 16pt right-glyph. Worth keeping in the toolkit: when a layout cramps because of a text qualifier, ask whether a glyph would do the same job.
- **Audit-verified subtext sizing — no change needed.** User flagged the en-route distance/duration subtext as potentially too small. Pulled Figma 825:3783's design context: subtext is 15pt Subheadline, *exactly* what we ship. No change. Worth noting that "looks small" perception sometimes reflects expectations from larger Apple/Google-Maps surfaces — Fresh Greens' bottom sheet keeps the Apple HIG subheadline scale intentionally, since it sits in a denser modal-content register, not a full-screen route summary.
- **Audits get steadily lighter when per-PR hygiene holds.** #1 caught major drift; #4 was already lighter; #5 was almost entirely stale comments; #6 is one targeted ETA fix. The audit cadence still has value as a baseline reset, but the cumulative drift you're auditing against has dropped close to zero. Worth reading as confirmation that the slop-trim discipline is paying compounding interest.

---

## feat/en-route-polish (2026-05-07)

Bottom-sheet polish on /en-route + a shared `lib/format.ts` for trip duration and distance strings used across /home and /en-route. Also brings /en-route's community-report rendering up to /home's MapMarker pattern (the leftover Circle rendering had been creating a yellow halo around the user's location whenever a caution-zone test report was logged near current position) and makes /home's destination underline conditional on "recurring trip" intent.

- **A "what is that yellow circle?" question is often a signal of cross-screen drift.** /home shipped MapMarker rendering for community reports in #50; /en-route still rendered the same data as wide caution Circles. Same data, two visual languages, three weeks apart in build order. Lesson: when a feature ships on screen A, sweep for "where else is this rendered?" before closing the PR. The audit cadence catches it eventually but the user notices first.
- **`formatDuration`/`formatDistance` belong in their own module the moment two screens share them.** Considered inlining since each formatter is ~5 lines, but /home and /en-route both render the same trip's duration + distance — drift between them ("65 min" on /home vs "1 hr 5 min" on /en-route) would read as a bug, not a feature. The rule of three usually says "inline twice, extract on the third" but cross-screen consistency-of-rendering is a stronger pull than line-count parsimony. `lib/format.ts` joins `lib/scoring.ts` and `lib/daylight.ts` as pure-function utilities.
- **Hard-code-false a feature flag when the dependency hasn't shipped yet.** Destination underline is meant to invite "save as home/work" for recurring trips. Without trip history (queued for `feat/recent-trips`), there's no way to *compute* recurrence — but the conditional rendering belongs in /home now so the flip-on is a one-line change later. Pattern: name the boolean (`isRegularDestination`), default it to `false`, and document the dependency in a comment. Better than coming back later and grafting the conditional in around live UI.
- **"Set once, forget" controls go to the screen edge.** Volume started rightmost in the en-route bottom-sheet utility row — Apple/Google Maps put their speaker toggles at the edge for the same reason. After walking the layout, the bigger lesson surfaced: auxiliary controls actually belong in the *side button column* (with Help/Shield/Report/Center) where the rest of the driving utilities live, not in the bottom-sheet row. Moved Volume to the top of the side column, leaving the bottom sheet symmetric (`[Search] [ETA] [Paths]`). The asymmetry of 1-icon-left + 2-icons-right wasn't landing — solving it by *moving the icon to where it semantically belongs* beats solving it by shrinking icons or adding hitSlop, because consistency of icon size across the row is its own design rule.

---

## chore/safety-button-rewire (2026-05-07)

Untwists the placeholder safety wiring across /home and /en-route. /home's top-left hamburger now opens /menu (was TEMP-wired to /safety). /en-route's side-button shield switches from a green Ionicons placeholder to the Figma-faithful Phosphor `Shield` (`weight="duotone"`, `color={colors.navy}`). Adds `colors.navy: '#041E49'` to the palette as the canonical safety-affordance blue.

- **Sample colors from screenshots when Figma's metadata is sparse.** The en-route frame's Figma node returned empty metadata (just bounding box, no children listed). Rather than chase down the right sub-node, downloaded the parent screenshot and ran a PIL pixel scan over the side-button column's y-band — found `#041E49` (deep navy) and `#a8c7fa` (light accent) in two passes. Worth knowing for any "what hex is this in Figma" question — when the design context tool isn't useful, a 5-line PIL script over a screenshot beats a 10-call Figma drill-down.
- **Reserved-color rule promotion → token addition.** `.cursorrules` lists Navy as a reserved safety signal, but the palette didn't have a navy token until this PR (the Officer/Trooper illustration just used `#1B3F8B` baked into the PNG). Adding `colors.navy` puts the safety-affordance blue under the same token discipline as orange/red/yellow. Lesson: when a documented reserved color earns its first real UI use, that's the right moment to tokenize it — not at "ship the design system" time, when it would have been speculative.
- **Phosphor `weight="duotone"` reads as Figma's two-tone icons for free.** Figma authors the en-route shield with two stops (`#041E49` outline + `#a8c7fa` fill). Phosphor's duotone weight derives the lighter accent automatically from the base color via opacity, which lands close enough to Figma's two-stop look that no manual second-color prop is needed. Worth knowing when the Figma source is two-stop and the Ionicons substitute is too flat.
- **Redundant menu entries are fine when the affordances mean different things.** /home now has two paths to /menu: the hamburger (top-left) and the Car-glyph avatar (right). Tempted to remove one, but they read as different invitations — hamburger says "open the drawer," avatar says "this is you." Waze, Apple Maps, and Google Maps all maintain similar redundancy. Lesson: redundancy in nav affordances is a feature when the *role* of each entry is distinct, even if the destination is the same.

---

## feat/map-markers-and-edge-indicators (2026-05-07)

Replaces /home's community-report Circles with custom Phosphor-glyph Markers, adds a saved-home Marker (long-press the map → Alert confirms → persisted to AsyncStorage), and renders EdgeIndicator pills on the screen edge for any POI outside the current viewport. Off-screen indicators tap-to-recenter the map on the POI.

- **Off-viewport indicators belong in screen-space, not the map's native layer.** First instinct was to keep all map UI inside `<MapView>`'s children. EdgeIndicators don't have a lat/lng — they live at viewport-edge pixel coordinates that change as the user pans. Solution: render them in an absolute-positioned `<View pointerEvents="box-none">` *over* the map, driven by `mapRegion` state (set on `onRegionChangeComplete`) and viewport size from the MapView's `onLayout`. The math (bearing + ray-rectangle intersection) lives in `lib/edge-indicators.ts` as pure functions, mirroring `lib/scoring.ts` and `lib/daylight.ts`. Worth knowing: when a UI overlay needs viewport coords rather than map coords, lift it out of the MapView entirely.
- **`tracksViewChanges={false}` prevents marker re-render thrash on iOS.** react-native-maps re-renders custom-children Markers on every pan/zoom unless this prop is set. With 5+ markers visible the lag was perceptible. Setting `tracksViewChanges={false}` on each `<Marker>` keeps the rendered output stable until the marker's coordinate or children actually change. Trade-off: dynamic content inside the marker (e.g. an animated pulse) would freeze too — fine for our static glyph case.
- **Long-press + `Alert.alert` is the right v1 UX for "save this location".** Skipped building a custom confirm sheet — `Alert.alert` is the iOS-native pattern, takes zero new components, and gives the user a real "are you sure" beat before persisting. Cancellation is free. The cleaner v2 (custom bottom sheet with map preview) ships when there's a real reason to deviate; for v1, native confirmation is the right anti-slop call.
- **Counter-rotation keeps icons upright in a rotating wrapper.** EdgeIndicator's pill rotates with the bearing toward the POI so its arrow tip points outward, but the glyph inside should stay readable (House up, Megaphone up). Wrapping the children in `<View style={{ transform: [{ rotate: `${-rotation}deg` }] }}>` cancels out the parent's rotation. Standard trick for compass-style UIs, worth keeping in the toolkit.
- **`isPointInRegion` is a literal lat/lng box check, not a haversine distance.** Tempted to use the distance-from-center formula, but the visible region is a rectangle in lat/lng space (not a circle), so a box check is both faster and correct. The math: `lat ∈ [center.lat - delta/2, center.lat + delta/2]`, same for lng. Worth knowing for anything that asks "is this point in the visible map" — RN-maps' `Region` already gives you the deltas, no haversine needed.
- **Sign-out cleanup needs to grow with new identity-attached state.** Adding `useSavedPlaces` introduced a new AsyncStorage key tied to the user. Without updating `/menu`'s `handleSignOut`, the next sign-in (potentially a different Apple ID) would inherit the previous user's saved home. Easy to forget — the sign-out function lives in a different file from where the new state was added. Worth noting as a project convention: every new "user-scoped" adapter must be added to `handleSignOut`'s `Promise.all` cleanup.

---

## chore/figma-fidelity-audit-5 (2026-05-07)

Fifth audit after 6 feature PRs landed since #4 (auth, trusted contact, menu hub, menu rework, recordings, illustrations + cloud animation). Ran a static sweep for the documented drift patterns — hardcoded widths, raw hex colors, fontSize/fontWeight overrides, fix-trace comments, stale TODOs — then fixed the small wins inline. Lighter-touch audit than #4 because most recent PRs included self-review polish passes; the surviving drift was almost entirely **stale comments** rather than design-system violations.

- **Static sweep > screen-by-screen Figma diff for routine audits.** Pulling 12+ Figma frames and visually comparing is expensive (tokens + time) and mostly redundant when no design has changed since the last audit. A grep pass for the documented anti-patterns (`width: 374`, raw `#XXXXXX` outside theme, inline `fontWeight`, `// TODO: install <dep>`) catches 90% of real drift in seconds. Reserve full screen-by-screen Figma diffs for audits *after* a Figma update, not after a code-side feature run.
- **Stale TODOs accumulate even when tasks ship.** Both `home.tsx:404` ("TODO: install expo-linear-gradient") and `home.tsx:462` ("once we install a sun calculator") referenced deps that had been installed *and used* — `LinearGradient` rendering five lines below the first TODO, suncalc threading through `lib/daylight.ts`. The shipping commit didn't update the now-misleading TODO. Lesson: when finishing work that resolves a TODO, search for the exact phrase ("install X", "TODO: X") and either delete the comment or rewrite it to capture what's still genuinely outstanding.
- **Stale dimension comments outlive the code's responsiveness.** `get-started.tsx` had two comments referencing `width: 326` ("Content wrapper: width: 326", "fills contentInner's 326pt") long after the actual implementation was rewritten to `alignSelf: 'stretch'` for Pro Max responsiveness. The 326 was a Figma-baseline number that earned its place in early commits as documentation but became misleading when the implementation surpassed it. Lesson: when you flip a hardcoded value to a responsive pattern, audit the comments naming the old value — they become a trap for the next reader.
- **`.gitignore` for sibling repos in the same checkout.** Cloning the public design-system specimen *inside* the private fresh-greens checkout produced a persistent `fresh-greens-specimen/` untracked entry in `git status`. Added an explicit ignore rule with a comment pointing to the canonical location. Worth knowing: when you have two related repos and one ends up nested locally, the right answer is `.gitignore`, not "remember not to commit it" — `git add -A` would have swept it in eventually.
- **Audits get lighter as self-review-on-PR matures.** #1 and #2 caught major drift (token mismatches, hardcoded widths, ACLU copy errors). #4 was already lighter. #5 was almost entirely comment cleanup because recent PRs (`feat/illustrations`, `feat/welcome-cloud-animation`) shipped explicit `chore:` commits trimming slop *before* merge. The audit cadence stays valuable as a baseline-reset, but the per-audit fix volume has been declining — that's the system working.

---

## feat/welcome-cloud-animation (2026-05-06)

Splits the Welcome backdrop composite into individual layers so the clouds + wind elements can drift independently. Each animated element is wrapped in a `Drift` helper that runs an `Animated.loop(Animated.sequence([...]))` translateX oscillation with `Easing.inOut(Easing.sin)` and `useNativeDriver: true`.

- **Composite-vs-individual is a one-way door for animation.** The previous PR's single `welcome-backdrop.png` was the right call for v1 — exact Figma fidelity, one asset, simple layout. But you can't animate a sub-region of a baked PNG. The moment "make the clouds move" became a goal, the composite had to be torn apart into 14 individual layers (Vic PNG + 13 SVGs) all absolutely positioned per the Figma authored coords. Worth knowing for future "static composite OR animated layers" decisions: pick based on whether motion is in scope. If motion is plausible later, lean toward individual layers from the start to avoid the rework.
- **`useNativeDriver: true` on translateX is essentially free.** The 10 simultaneous looping animations (7 clouds + 3 winds) run on the UI thread without touching the JS bundle. No frame drops on the splash screen, no contention with `useUser`'s AsyncStorage read or the `router.replace('/home')` redirect during the cold-start path. Lesson: any time the only animated property is transform/opacity, set `useNativeDriver: true` — it's a strict perf upgrade with no downside.
- **Per-element duration variance > random phase offset for desync.** First instinct was to randomize each cloud's starting phase (`Animated.Value(Math.random() * amplitude)`) so they wouldn't move in lockstep at t=0. Settled instead on giving each cloud a different `duration` (2.8s–5.8s spread across the 10 elements) — the desync emerges naturally after the first cycle, and starting them all at offset 0 keeps the initial frame faithful to Figma's authored composition. Random phase would also break determinism (snapshot tests would flake). Worth knowing: when orchestrating multiple looping animations, varying their periods is enough to prevent visible synchronization, no random offset needed.
- **`Easing.inOut(Easing.sin)` reads as "ambient drift" specifically.** Linear easing makes clouds look like they're on rails. `Easing.inOut(Easing.cubic)` is too aggressive (clouds visibly slow at the edges). `Easing.inOut(Easing.sin)` is the closest analog to natural air drift — slows almost imperceptibly at the extrema, accelerates smoothly through the middle. Worth saving for any "subtle motion" use case where the goal is to make motion feel organic rather than digital.
- **390×846 reference frame, bottom-centered, on a wider device.** The backdrop scene is a fixed 390×846 container wrapped in `alignItems: 'center'` + `justifyContent: 'flex-end'`. On iPhone Pro Max (430-wide), there's ~20pt of empty orange on each side at the top corners — invisible because the orange backgrounds match the root color. On iPhone SE (smaller height), the scene gets clipped at the top (border cloud peeks off-screen) but the hill stays anchored at the bottom. Worth knowing: when you have a fixed-aspect illustrated scene that needs to pin to one edge across device sizes, bottom-aligning the entire scene gives the most predictable hill/horizon behavior.

---

## feat/illustrations (2026-05-06)

Ports four placeholder-Ionicons-or-CSS-shaped illustrations to real Figma exports: Officer + Trooper characters in /pulled-over's review, the three onboarding-panel hero illustrations, the location-pin + car on /permissions, and a single composite backdrop for /index (Welcome) replacing the previous Vic + sun + CSS-hill stack and adding the missing clouds, wind, and border-cloud elements.

- **Figma MCP asset URLs return SVG bodies despite `Content-Type: text/html`.** First instinct was the URLs were broken (HTML head/length suggested a wrapping page). `curl -s <url> | head -c 500` showed real `<svg>...` content. The mismatch is just bad server-side content negotiation — pipe and inspect, don't trust headers. Saved hours of "why aren't these downloading right."
- **One composite screenshot beats reassembling positioned children.** Welcome's "Visuals" parent (825:3163) contains a vic + sun + hill + 7 clouds + 3 winds + border-cloud at specific x/y offsets. Trying to reproduce all that absolute positioning faithfully in RN would have been a bug-magnet (every cloud's pixel offset to maintain). Instead, requesting a screenshot of the parent gave a single 390×846 PNG that became the entire Welcome backdrop. The content (title, terms, buttons) overlays via SafeAreaView. One image, zero positioning math, exact Figma fidelity. Same trick works for any frame with many absolutely-positioned decorative elements.
- **`sips --cropOffset Y X --cropToHeightWidth H W` — Y first, X second.** First crop attempt got mostly empty white because I passed `0 369` (treated as Y=0, X=369 → crops from x=369 onward, basically off-image). Fixed by swapping to `369 0`. Even after fixing the order, sips silently kept the source's full dimensions on output (output was 390×844 instead of 390×475). Switched to PIL via `python3 -m pip install --user --quiet Pillow` + `img.crop((left, upper, right, lower))` which actually produced the cropped pixels. Worth knowing: when an image-tool's command "succeeds" but the output dimensions don't match what you asked for, the tool likely interpreted your args differently — read the output dimensions, not the exit code.
- **Crop screenshots once, render with `aspectRatio` in RN.** Onboarding panels' illustrations were cropped to 390×351 from the Figma panel screenshots. In RN we use `aspectRatio: 390/351` plus `width: <screenWidth>` so the image scales proportionally on Pro Max devices (430-wide) without distortion. Don't hardcode `height: 351` — it breaks on wider devices. The aspectRatio-driven height pattern is the responsive default for any fixed-aspect image asset.
- **PNG export resolution is generous; don't worry about retina.** Officer + Trooper PNGs came back at 1384×2186 / 1384×2361 from Figma despite rendering at 100×157 / 100×171pt. Far over even @3x retina (300×471 max needed). `resizeMode: 'contain'` handles the downscale crisply on any device pixel ratio. Lesson: don't pre-scale Figma exports to "the right" pt size; let RN do the per-device math against an oversized source.
- **Layered SVG overlays positioned via percentage `inset` need pixel calculation in RN.** Trooper's hat badge was authored in Figma as a separate SVG positioned at `inset-[20.99%_43.22%_68.99%_43.23%]` within the 120×172 wrapper. In RN we don't have percentage `top/right/bottom/left` for absolute positioning the same way, so I converted to pixels: `top: 36 (= 20.99% × 172), left: 52 (= 43.23% × 120)`. Pre-compute these against the wrapper's known dimensions; don't try to use `top: '20.99%'` — RN's behavior with percentage absolute insets is inconsistent across versions.
- **Removing CSS-shaped placeholders is a real cleanup.** Welcome's hill was a `View` with `borderTopLeftRadius: 600` + huge negative `left/right` to fake a curve — a hack that worked for v0 but read as obvious approximation against the rest of the brand. Replacing with the real Figma-authored hill curve (now part of the composite backdrop) made the screen feel intentionally designed instead of "we'll fix this later." Worth noting for any other CSS-approximated illustrations in the codebase: they're flagged in CLAUDE.md's "Polish/smaller gaps" section, and each one is a quick win to swap.
- **Defer the user-car glyph until the asset exists.** CLAUDE.md flags a custom user-car SVG that the user is making themselves (variant of the trusted-friend pin). It's not in Figma, so this branch can't pull it. Skipped on this PR; remains a one-tile follow-up commit when `assets/illustrations/user-car.svg` lands. Lesson: when an illustration audit's scope includes "asset to be created," explicitly skip it on the porting PR rather than placing a proxy that has to be replaced again later.

---

## feat/recordings-list (2026-05-06)

Builds the audio library for /pulled-over's safety captures: `lib/api/recordings.ts` adapter, `useRecordings` hook, `/recordings` screen with inline playback + delete, /pulled-over auto-saving on dismiss. Plus a mid-stop "Add a contact" recovery affordance for users who skipped trusted-contact onboarding, and a defensive name-derivation fix for iOS contacts that return `name: undefined`.

- **expo-file-system v19 is class-based now.** First pass used `FileSystem.documentDirectory` + `FileSystem.copyAsync` (the v18 procedural API); type-check failed because those exports are gone. v19 uses `new Directory(Paths.document, 'recordings')`, `new File(...)`, `file.copy(destFile)`, `file.delete()` — and most ops are *synchronous*. Async-to-sync is rare in JS migrations; worth knowing because every "wait, why isn't this returning a promise" instinct is wrong here.
- **Async work in unmount cleanups races the host hook's own teardown.** First save attempt did `await recorder.stop()` inside a `useEffect(() => () => {...})` cleanup. By the time the await resolved, `useAudioRecorder`'s own cleanup had run and disposed the native shared object — accessing `recorder.uri` threw `NativeSharedObjectNotFoundException`. Lesson: never *await* cross-hook resources from inside an unmount cleanup. The cleanup is allowed to run, but you don't control when other hooks' cleanups run relative to yours.
- **`usePreventRemove` > `navigation.addListener('beforeRemove')` for native-stack.** beforeRemove + preventDefault works on JS-stack but in native-stack the gesture dismiss removes the screen natively before JS can hold it back — you get a "screen was removed natively but didn't get removed from JS state" warning. usePreventRemove (from `@react-navigation/native`) is the supported pattern for this case. It also takes a boolean gate so you can flip it off after the work completes and re-dispatch the original action without re-triggering the prevent.
- **Always normalize at the adapter boundary; don't trust source SDKs to give you string-typed strings.** iOS's expo-contacts returned a Contact with `name: undefined` (only firstName/lastName were set), and `useTrustedContact.pickContact` passed it straight to `setTrustedContact({ name: picked.name, ... })`. TypeScript's `name: string` was satisfied in the call site (typed as `Contact['name']` which is optional) but the runtime stored undefined. `deriveContactName(name, firstName, lastName, phoneNumber)` now derives a real string before persistence. Worth knowing: SDK-typed-as-optional fields *will* be undefined for your edge-case users; build the fallback chain at the adapter, not the UI.
- **Mid-stress recovery affordances earn their keep even if rare.** The "no trusted contact set" state on /pulled-over previously rendered "?" initials + "No trusted contact set" + disabled Call/Text — a literal dead end at the worst moment. Replacing the avatar block with a tappable "Add a contact" affordance (UserPlus glyph, no pulse — pulsing on an empty avatar would lie about a connection) lets a user who skipped onboarding recover even mid-stop. Compromise: yes, opening an iOS contact picker during a stop is suboptimal. But "out of luck" is a much worse failure mode than "extra friction." Lesson: when shipping a flow that depends on prior setup, audit every "user skipped that setup" path and at minimum offer a recovery affordance. Hiding the feature behind onboarding alone is a bug magnet.
- **Defensive UI fallbacks pay off when data shape changes mid-development.** Stored contacts saved with `name: undefined` don't disappear when the adapter learns to derive names — they sit in AsyncStorage until cleared. ContactView's `contact?.name?.trim() || contact?.phoneNumber || NO_CONTACT_NAME` chain handles all three cases (real name, broken stored contact, no contact) without stranding the user on a contact they can't re-pick. Worth knowing: when you change a data-shape contract, the UI defends against the *old* shape until users churn through; pure adapter-level fixes leave existing-data users stuck.
- **Recordings library = artifact of safety flow, not top-level setting.** First instinct put Recordings as its own row in /menu's settings list. User pushed back: it's a safety-flow artifact, not a global preference, so it belongs under Safety Settings next to Trusted Contact. Lesson on info architecture: list a thing where its *origin* lives, not where the user might first look. /recordings exists *because* of /pulled-over; surfacing it under Safety keeps that lineage clear.
- **`useRecordings()` is per-instance state, not shared.** Each call to the hook creates its own `useState` cell. /pulled-over's instance saves a recording → its local state updates → its parent (about to unmount) doesn't care. /recordings's *separate* instance refetches on next mount (expo-router stack remounts on re-visit), so it sees the new entry. Worth knowing: this works *because* of stack-based remounting; if /recordings ever stays mounted (tabs, persistent route), we'd need either context-shared state or a focus refetch. Document the assumption (mount = fresh fetch) so the next refactor doesn't quietly break it.

---

## feat/menu-hub (2026-05-06)

Third "make it real" PR. The Settings spine that hosts secondary surfaces — `/menu` route pushed from /home's avatar button, hero header with car-icon identity glyph, trusted-contact edit, sign out. Sets the visual register for Recordings + Recent Trips when they arrive.

- **Asking for design input mid-build is sometimes the right move.** Started building /menu with iOS-Settings conventions (gray background, all-caps section labels, red Sign out). Halfway through, the user flagged they wanted to be more collaborative since there was no Figma. Surfacing the eight design decisions I'd already made — and which leans I had on each — produced a tighter design conversation than just shipping my first guess. Lesson: when extrapolating without a reference, *show the calls you're making* before completing the build, not after. The user gets to redirect cheaply; we both end up with a design we agreed on instead of one we have to revisit.
- **Brand register beats system convention when they conflict.** iOS Settings uses gray + uppercase tracked-out section labels + red destructive text. Fresh Greens' established register is wiltedgreen + Title1Emphasized + reserved-color rules where red = safety signal (not destructive action). Trying to match iOS would have made /menu read as a stranger room compared to every other auth/onboarding screen. Going wiltedgreen + Title1Emphasized + wiltedgreen-outline-pill Sign out kept the visual continuity. The user pushes from /home (white) to /menu (wiltedgreen) and into /trusted-contact-setup (wiltedgreen) without any color-temperature whiplash. Lesson: the established system trumps the OS convention when they collide. The OS can be referenced; the brand has to be honored.
- **One identity glyph across surfaces > "wherever the user is, show their data."** The trusted-friend pin on /home uses car iconography (per Figma). Extending the same car to the user's own avatar — at /home (28pt) AND /menu hero (80pt) — makes "user is a car-in-the-system" a coherent metaphor everywhere. Differentiated colors (freshgreen for trusted friend, fadedgreen for self) signal role within the same visual language. The first version of /menu used initials in a circle — functional but bureaucratic, no identity story. Cars-everywhere reads more like the app actually believes its own thesis ("drive like you know these roads").
- **A tsconfig `paths` mapping pays off twice.** First use was avoiding the type-check failure on phosphor's source TSX in /trusted-contact-setup. Second use is here in /menu and /home — same pattern, no extra config. The pattern is now portable to every future Phosphor icon use across the app. Worth knowing: when a third-party package's deep-import path is the right runtime answer but breaks type-check, tsconfig `paths` is the cleanest solution. Don't bury the suppression in `@ts-expect-error` per call site.
- **The dev Reset chip retired itself once Sign out shipped.** Removing one-off dev affordances when their permanent replacement lands is part of "real" — leaving them around as long-tail debug clutter is what makes a product feel like it has tools half-built. The chip lasted exactly the time between "Welcome auto-redirects so I need a sign-out somewhere" and "Settings exists, sign-out is in its real home." That's the right lifecycle for any `__DEV__` workaround: introduce when needed, remove when the real version ships.
- **Routing ambiguity is solved with explicit intent params, not stack inspection.** /trusted-contact-setup is reached from two places now: onboarding (where Continue should `replace('/home')`) and Settings (where Continue should `back()` to /menu). Trying to detect the entry point from the route stack would be brittle (what if the user deep-links someday?). The clean answer is a `from=settings` query param the entry-point route passes — explicit, debuggable, easy to extend with new entry points (`from=onboarding-resume`, etc). Lesson: when a screen serves multiple parent flows, let the parents declare themselves. Don't ask the screen to introspect.
- **Future direction documented in CLAUDE.md, not just in heads.** The Waze-flavored "tile grid + card-forward + identity-prominent" direction for /menu growth got written into CLAUDE.md so the next PR (Recordings or Recent Trips) starts from that direction instead of re-deriving from scratch. Lesson: when an aesthetic direction gets agreed on but isn't built yet, *write it down where the next PR will read it*. The conversation context where we agreed will be gone by then.
- **Designing without a Figma reference burns iterations.** First /menu commit shipped, user walked it on Expo Go, then we did several rounds of rework: hero scale, section label hierarchy, "Settings" page-title removal, accordion-pattern for the Zone toggle, row gap tightening, white-circle-vs-no-fill icon treatment, rectangular tile pivot, swipe-with-peek carousel, sign-out pinned to bottom, in-tile copy lifted from Figma Search Landing instead of generic placeholders. Each round was a real improvement, but each was a tank of iteration cost the design system couldn't pre-empt. Honest read for next time: when there's no Figma to anchor to, **expect 3–5 iterations on visual polish even after structure lands**. The collaboration only works if both sides know that going in.
- **`react-native-svg-transformer` makes Figma SVG → first-class component cheap.** Setup: install the dev dep, add 7 lines to `metro.config.js` (filter `assetExts`, add to `sourceExts`, set `babelTransformerPath`), declare `*.svg` module in a `.d.ts`. Result: `import FuelIcon from '...fuel.svg'` and the SVG is a real RN component that scales to any size. Worth knowing this is a 5-minute one-time setup. Once configured, every future custom illustration is `import` away — no more juggling `@1x`/`@2x`/`@3x` PNGs or fighting Image's resize math. The 32×32 PNG hold-over felt soft at 48pt; the same SVG looks crisp at any size.
- **Pinned-to-bottom layout = put the pinned thing OUTSIDE the ScrollView.** First /menu version had Sign out as the last child *inside* the ScrollView, which means it sat right under the last visible row and didn't get pushed to the bottom of the screen. Fix: ScrollView gets `flex: 1` (fills the space between header and pinned bottom), Sign out is a sibling of the ScrollView underneath, SafeAreaView's bottom edge handles the inset. When the carousel needed the same treatment, same pattern applied — pinned-to-bottom things live OUTSIDE the scrollable content. The tell that you're doing it wrong: the pinned thing's position depends on how much content is above it.
- **Subtitle copy from a real Figma frame > generic placeholder.** Original carousel tiles read "Set up" as the value line. After lifting the Search Landing's Fuel-section subtitle ("Add your car's model and fuel for refuel reminders") and writing the Notifications subtitle in the same register, the tiles felt purposeful instead of stub-y. Lesson: if the design system already has copy for adjacent surfaces, use that voice/register before inventing. The tone is part of the design, not just the layout.
- **A `Preferences` adapter scales better than per-feature constants.** `SHOW_ZONES` started as a `const` in home.tsx for thesis screenshots. As soon as it became a real user-visible toggle, it needed to live in storage with a typed shape that other prefs can join (theme, auto-record, distance units, etc.). Built `lib/api/preferences.ts` + `hooks/usePreferences.ts` matching the user/trusted-contact adapter pattern. One storage key, one merged Preferences object, per-key setters. Adding a new preference is now a one-property change to the type plus a setter. Worth knowing for any "this seems like just a debug constant for now" moment — the path from constant to real preference is foreseeable; build the adapter at the second use, not the third.

---

## feat/trusted-contact-end-to-end (2026-05-06)

Second "make it real" PR after Apple Sign In. Adds the trusted-contact picker (iOS-native via `expo-contacts`), wires the pulled-over contact phase to read it, and turns Call/Text into real `Linking.openURL('tel:…')` / `sms:…` actions. Also moves mic permission from mid-stress (during /pulled-over) to calm onboarding.

- **`presentContactPickerAsync()` is the right pick over `getContactsAsync` for v1.** The OS-native picker doesn't require the Contacts permission — the user picks one contact, only that contact comes back. We get a real native UX (familiar iOS picker, same as iMessage/Phone use), no permission dialog, no "show all contacts in our own UI" rabbit hole. Picker-style flows beat permission-then-list flows when the data scope is "one item I'm choosing right now."
- **Adapter shape stayed identical to user.ts.** Same `getX / setX / clearX` async surface, same AsyncStorage backing, same backend-swap-point comment. The third adapter using this pattern (zones / community-reports / user / trusted-contact = four now) means it's a real codebase convention, not a one-off. Future identity-style adapters (recordings, recent trips) should slot into the same shape.
- **Store only what the safety flow needs.** Contacts on iOS can be huge — addresses, social profiles, photos, all the postal labels. Caching the full Contact would be both privacy-sloppy and storage-wasteful. We extract `id`, `name`, `initials` (derived at save), `phoneNumber` (primary or first), `setAt` and persist that. The OS contact remains the source-of-truth; we cache the *projection* the app actually uses.
- **Fallbacks across denial paths matter.** Three places needed graceful degradation:
  - Skip during onboarding → no trusted contact → `/pulled-over` Call/Text buttons render *visible-but-disabled* (opacity 0.4, accessibilityState disabled, no-op handlers). Not hidden — hiding implies the feature doesn't exist; disabled implies "you can use this once you set it up."
  - Mic permission denied during onboarding → recording metering is undefined → waveform stays at flat baseline (already shipped behavior in audit-4's recording work; just inherits the fallback).
  - Picked contact has no phone number → `pickContact` throws a typed error caught in setup screen → user sees "Pick a different contact." rather than a silent failure.
- **Permission timing is part of UX.** Mic was originally requested during /pulled-over's guidance phase — the *worst* moment to interrupt with a system dialog. Moving the request to /permissions during calm onboarding lets the user grant in advance; the safety flow stays silent about it. Lesson: any permission that affects a critical-moment feature should be requested at a non-critical moment. If your app needs camera-during-emergency, ask for camera at signup, not at the emergency.
- **`tel:` / `sms:` URI schemes are platform-agnostic and reliable.** No platform-specific code needed; `Linking.openURL('tel:+1...')` works on iOS and Android. The number's format barely matters — both accept formatted (`(212) 555-0123`) and unformatted (`2125550123`). Worth knowing this isn't a fragile area; the brittle thing in real-call wiring is *getting* the number, not *using* it.
- **"Set-and-forget" defers the editable-Settings problem.** Trusted contact is set during onboarding only; there's no "edit my trusted contact" flow yet. That's a deliberate Plan A scope decision (Settings is its own next PR). It works because thesis-demo users go through onboarding once. The lesson is granular: when a feature has a setup phase and a use phase, you can ship just the setup phase + use phase first, and defer the *edit* phase until you have user feedback on what edit affordances actually need to exist.
- **Permission asks back-to-back are OK on iOS.** Originally I worried about "two dialogs in a row" feeling intrusive at /permissions (location then mic). It actually reads fine — iOS treats them as separate, sequential prompts; the user understands each one. The key is they're both contextually justified by the same screen ("we need these for routing + safety recording"). Not lumping them into a single "grant everything" prompt.
- **Onboarding momentum > permission gating.** /permissions originally dead-ended at iOS Settings on denial — the user had to leave the app, toggle the permission, swipe back, and tap again. That's a stall in the middle of a one-time flow. Better: always advance after the prompts fire, regardless of grant/deny. The app degrades gracefully without either permission. Users who denied can grant later via system Settings; they don't need to grant *right now* to keep moving. Lesson: onboarding should never have a "you can't continue without this" wall unless the feature is genuinely useless without it.
- **Phosphor's barrel index breaks Metro at scale.** `import { UserPlus } from 'phosphor-react-native'` triggers Metro to bundle the barrel — which re-exports ~9000 icons. Any single icon's resolution failure (we hit `./icons/Bank` for unclear reasons) crashes the entire bundle. Fix: deep-import via `phosphor-react-native/src/icons/UserPlus`. Metro now bundles only the one icon. Side-effect: TypeScript follows the deep `.tsx` source instead of the barrel's `.d.ts` and starts type-checking phosphor's strict-incompatible internals (`className` on Svg). `skipLibCheck` doesn't help because it skips `.d.ts`, not `.tsx` from `node_modules`. Real fix: tsconfig `paths` mapping that redirects TS to phosphor's precompiled `.d.ts` while Metro continues using the source. Two-tool, two-paths solution: TS uses `lib/typescript/icons/*`, Metro uses `src/icons/*`. Worth knowing this gotcha before reaching for any icon library that ships a giant barrel.
- **Dev affordances belong on a reachable screen.** Wanted to test cold-start onboarding repeatedly without deleting the simulator each time. But Welcome auto-redirects signed-in users — there's no surface a logged-in tester can reach to sign out. Solution: a `__DEV__`-gated "Reset (dev)" chip on Home (the place signed-in users land). Stripped from production builds automatically by Metro. Lesson: every "reset" or "logout" affordance has to consider the bootstrap problem — what screen can the user in the BAD state actually see?

---

## feat/auth-apple-signin (2026-05-06)

First "make it real" PR after the audit. Wires Apple Sign In via `expo-apple-authentication`, adds the missing `/login` screen, and introduces the user-identity adapter (`lib/api/user.ts`) + reactive hook (`hooks/useUser.ts`).

- **Adapter pattern carries to identity.** `lib/api/user.ts` follows the same shape the zones / community-reports / routes adapters use — typed `User`, async public surface (`getStoredUser`, `setStoredUser`, `clearStoredUser`, `upsertUser`), AsyncStorage internals as the v1 backing store, with the backend-swap point clearly marked. Identity now flows through the same architectural register as everything else, which is the win: a future Supabase swap is a single-file change, not a cross-screen refactor. The thesis claim of a typed-adapter pipeline now holds for auth too, not just data.
- **Apple's first-sign-in-only behavior is a real architectural concern.** Apple returns `fullName` and `email` only on the user's *first* sign-in to an app. Returning sign-ins return only the stable `user` ID. If you naively persist what Apple gives you each time, returning users overwrite their cached display name with nulls. `upsertUser` solves this by merging — newest-wins, but `null` doesn't overwrite a real value. This is the kind of provider quirk that's easy to miss until a returning user shows up with their initials reading "?".
- **Routing decisions belong at the call site, not in the auth hook.** `useUser`'s `signInWithApple` returns the freshly-stored User and stops. The caller (`/get-started` vs `/login`) decides where to navigate post-sign-in — first-time → `/onboarding`, returning → `/home`, login screen always → `/home`. Hooks shouldn't have opinions about flow; they should have opinions about state. Keeping the hook flow-agnostic means it slots into Settings sign-out / re-auth later without rewiring.
- **First-time vs returning is checked from storage, not from Apple's response.** Apple's `signInAsync` doesn't tell you whether this is the user's first time on this app. `getStoredUser()` *before* the sign-in does — if there's already a user record, this is a returning sign-in. Simple, source-of-truth-y, doesn't depend on provider quirks.
- **Auto-redirect needs a `loading` guard.** Welcome's auto-redirect for signed-in users (`if (!loading && user) router.replace('/home')`) requires waiting on the AsyncStorage read. Without the guard, `user` is briefly `null` on cold start even when a user IS stored, and Welcome would either flash and bounce, or worse, leave a signed-in user staring at the splash. The pattern is: don't navigate on absence-of-information, only on confirmed-information.
- **Auth + login as one PR is the right granularity.** Splitting them would have meant Welcome → /login routing nowhere meaningful for a stretch, OR a half-built login screen sitting in code without a destination. Bundling the two means main always has a working flow — the user can sign up, sign in, return, and re-enter the app coherently from the moment this lands. The "next PR" pattern (this one's a foundation; trusted-contact picker + recordings list build on top) keeps each branch small and testable while never landing a half-feature.
- **Visual reuse held off on extraction.** /login is a near-clone of /get-started's layout but routes differently and has different copy. Resisting the urge to extract a shared `<AuthScreen />` component before there's a third use — applying the rule of three. When Settings ships with a sign-in surface, that'll be the third use, and *then* the shared component will know what its real shape needs to be.
- **`usesAppleSignIn: true` in app.json + the plugin install + the entitlement = three things that all have to land together.** Forgetting any one breaks the sign-in sheet at runtime in ways the dev hasn't seen since the previous Expo SDK migration. Worth a one-time mental note: every iOS-entitlement feature has this three-part dance.

---

## chore/figma-fidelity-audit-4 (2026-05-06)

Fourth fidelity audit after the pulled-over consolidation (heavy structural change). Scope: token drift, responsive sizing, audit-3 baseline regressions, cross-flow pulse coherence, and a new dimension this audit — comparing the pulled-over legal copy against ACLU's published "Stopped by Police" guidance.

- **Recurring miss returns: hitSlop=8 in the search bar.** Audit-3 standardized at hitSlop=12 for modal-header dismiss patterns. The search-bar internal buttons (back chevron, mic) are the same pattern but were missed — a 24pt icon with hitSlop=8 only reaches 40pt total, below the 44pt HIG floor. Fix is one-line, but the lesson is the pattern: every audit catches the *same* hitSlop class of miss in a slightly different place. Worth a `grep -rn "hitSlop={8}"` as a literal pre-commit check on every PR going forward.
- **Token drift hides in `rgba()` strings.** Six different inline rgba values across the codebase (modal scrim, card border, separator, fills, drag-handle bar, divider) — none of them brand colors, all of them semantic roles that should live in `theme/colors.ts`. Inline rgba reads as "ad hoc" because it doesn't look like a design choice; it looks like a quick-typed value. Tokenizing them (`modalScrim`, `cardBorderSubtle`, `separatorSubtle`, `fillsTertiary`, `dragHandleBar`, `dividerNeutral`) made the same role explicitly named in one place. Habit going forward: if a value is `rgba(...)` and not a one-off shadow, it's a token, not a literal.
- **Hardcoded button widths fail on Pro Max.** Pattern audit-3 caught for menu rows showed up here for welcome and get-started auth buttons (326pt) and the report popup card (351pt). On 390pt iPhone they read as designed; on 430pt Pro Max they orphan with 50pt gutters instead of 32pt. Fix: `alignSelf: 'stretch'` + parent `paddingHorizontal: 32`. The popup keeps a `maxWidth: 400` so on tablet/landscape it doesn't go past dialog-feel. Lesson: if a width is a 3-digit number derived from "Figma's 390pt baseline minus margins," it's a responsive bug waiting to surface — express the *margins* in code, not the *width*.
- **`usePulseOpacity` extracted to `hooks/`.** TrustedContactStatus had its own inline tween; pulled-over's hook was the same tween parameterized. Three pulses (trusted-contact dot, recording chip, avatar ring) were running on duplicated implementations of the same rhythm. One shared hook now. The hook takes a `minOpacity` arg so depth varies by surface size while rhythm stays constant — a subtle concept (rhythm-as-system, depth-as-context) that's easier to spec when there's one place defining the rhythm.
- **Daylight gradient anchors tokenized.** The `#FFB347 / #C4785A / #2D1B69` palette was inlined in two places (legend on /home, polyline in lib/daylight.ts) — audit-3 unified the colors but left them inline. Now they're `colors.daylightDawn / Dusk / Night`. Documented daylight-encoding exception per cursorrules, but tokenizing makes the legend↔polyline relationship explicit by *shared name* rather than shared values. Easier to keep aligned on the next change.
- **Legal copy comparison is its own audit dimension.** First audit to compare app copy against the canonical legal source (ACLU's "Stopped by Police" guidance). Found six issues, four recommended fixes:
  - "*right to remain silent*" replacing the fuzzier "answer questions beyond identifying yourself"
  - paired clarification that documents (license/registration/insurance) are still required even when staying silent
  - **right to record** added to *What to Know* — ACLU explicitly affirms it, and the app is doing exactly that during the guidance phase, so omitting it from the rights review was a thesis-level inconsistency
  - **right to leave if not under arrest** added — concrete ACLU-listed post-stop guidance
  - firearm bullet split: a clean quotable script (no awkward `[location of firearm]` placeholder) + a separate non-quoted instruction about disclosure timing. The bracket placeholder read as unrendered template text on a high-stress screen, which is the worst time to ask the user to mentally fill in a variable.
- **Bullet count drove a layout fix.** *What to Know* went from 3 bullets → 6, which combined with the 320pt illustration overflows iPhone 14's modal height. Wrapping `ContentView` in a `ScrollView` (with `flexGrow: 1` instead of `flex: 1` on the content container) handles overflow gracefully without changing the visual when content fits. Lesson: when you suspect a content-driven overflow risk on a list-of-bullets layout, default to ScrollView. The cost is near-zero; the protection is real.
- **WebFetch can't reach the live ACLU page** (SPA-rendered, content not in raw HTML). Worked from canonical published-knowledge phrasing this round; the fix-or-not-fix calls were surfaced for explicit sign-off rather than auto-applied. Going forward: when verifying app copy against an authoritative source that's behind client-side rendering, plan to read the source manually before the audit instead of relying on WebFetch.

## feat/recording-contact (2026-05-06)

Consolidated the four-screen pulled-over flow (armed-or-not → recording → contact → review-guidance) into a single state-machine modal at `/pulled-over`. Added live audio recording with mic-driven waveform, expo-speech for "Read aloud", and a persistent recording indicator across phases.

- **Modal stacking depth was a real UX cost.** Five swipe-downs to peel back to /home after a stressful event was a structural drag, not a polish item. Collapsing four sibling routes into one modal with internal phases (armed → transition → guidance → contact → review) returns the dismissal to one swipe. The state-machine pattern was already proven inside `/review-guidance` (5 sub-views, currentIndex); extending it modal-wide was the natural next move. Big-feeling problem, small-feeling refactor — that asymmetry usually signals you're solving the right thing.
- **`useEffect` keyed on a phase value will re-fire each transition.** First implementation re-called `prepareToRecordAsync()` + `record()` on every phase change; iOS throws the second time and the recorder dies silently. Fix: a ref-backed "exactly once" latch (`hasStartedRecordingRef`). When start logic must run once but the trigger condition is part of normal state churn, refs are the right tool — `useEffect` deps alone can't express "first time only."
- **`durationMillis` is a more reliable heartbeat than `metering` for the metering-history poll.** Initially keyed the buffer-update effect on `recorderState.metering`. When metering was undefined or didn't change, the effect didn't re-run and the waveform stalled. Switched to `recorderState.durationMillis` — monotonic while recording, ticks every poll interval, guaranteed cadence. Lesson: when an effect needs to run *on a clock*, depend on a value that changes *with the clock*, not a value that changes *with the data*.
- **Pulse opacity range scales with surface size.** Reused TrustedContactStatus's tween (opacity 1↔0.3, 800ms each) for the avatar ring. The dot version reads as a heartbeat at 8pt; on a 160pt ring the same range reads as a strobe. Fix: parameterize `usePulseOpacity` with a `minOpacity` arg (default 0.3 preserves the dot, avatar passes 0.55). Same rhythm, different depth. Reusing animation primitives requires accounting for what the surface itself contributes to the perception.
- **Animating opacity cascades through children.** First version wrapped the entire concentric-ring stack in an `Animated.View` and animated its opacity — the middle ring + initials all faded with the outer ring. Fix: restructure to a non-animated parent (`avatarStack`) holding the static middle-ring + filled-circle as a flow child, with the animated outer ring as an absolute-positioned overlay sibling. To pulse one layer of a stack, the animated element has to be a *sibling*, not a *parent*. RN's opacity inheritance is the trap.
- **Don't promise nav destinations that don't exist.** First copy on the recording footnote was "Saved to your account · view in Settings." There's no Settings screen, no account, no recordings list. The "view in Settings" half made the copy a placeholder admission rather than a statement. Softened to "Recordings are saved to your account" — a fact, not a promise. Lesson: when in doubt about whether a nav target will land, write the copy as a *statement* until the destination exists.
- **Persistent indicator > "trust us" silence.** Removed the stop button (recording is ambient protection, not user-managed). But that left contact + review phases with no visual evidence of recording at all — a black box. Added a `RecordingChip` (pulse dot + label + timer) at the top of those phases. Same `usePulseOpacity` rhythm as the trusted-contact dot, different color (red vs freshgreen). Three pulses across the flow now (trusted contact, avatar ring, recording chip) — different surfaces, one heartbeat. The shared rhythm makes it read as a coherent system instead of three competing animations.
- **`fontVariant: ['tabular-nums']` is the right move on any ticking digit.** Without it, proportional figures shift width as digits change (8s wider than 1s) and the timer jiggles every second. Easy to miss in design review until you watch the timer for ~10 seconds and notice the second-digit dancing. Adopted on both the recording-widget timer and the persistent recording chip.
- **Big visible deliverable + heavy structural change = audit due.** This branch deleted four routes, added two dependencies (expo-audio, expo-speech), introduced a new state-machine pattern, and re-rendered five Figma nodes. Per workflow: any heavy structural PR triggers the next fidelity audit independent of the ~5 PR cadence. `chore/figma-fidelity-audit-4` queued for after merge.

---

## feat/routing-formula-zones (2026-05-06)

The remaining three thesis factors — police, wildlife, road conditions — land as OSM-fed zones through the existing pipeline. What we did, in plain terms:

- **The discriminated union earned its keep again.** Adding three new categories to the routing formula meant: extend the Overpass query union, add new tag dispatchers in the parser, add new entries in mock fallback. **No change** required to `pickWinner`'s public signature, no consumer changes in /home or /en-route, no scoring inner-loop rewrite. Discriminated `geometry: 'polygon' | 'polyline' | 'point'` made the geometry side trivial; adding optional `category: ZoneCategory` made per-source modulation possible without touching the type system everywhere else. Three new data sources, ~200 lines of net adapter additions, zero ripples.
- **Time modulation belongs in scoring, not in the zones adapter.** The dawn/dusk wildlife multiplier could've been baked into the zone (compute "is it dusk" at fetch time, return type='avoid' instead of 'caution'). Doing it in scoring is cleaner: zones describe *what's there*; scoring decides *what to do about it given the trip context*. Trip context (departureTime) lives at the call site of `pickWinner`, not at the time of fetching zones — and a route may be re-scored against the same zones for different scheduled departures (the future "Schedule for 7:38 AM" feature). Single source of zone truth, multiple scorings as needed.
- **Mixed-geometry Overpass queries (way + node) need both element types in the response parser.** OSM's `amenity=police` exists as both nodes (point markers) and ways (building polygons); `highway=speed_camera` and `hazard=wildlife_crossing` are always nodes. Parser used to assume `way` only — needed to add `parseOverpassNode` and a top-level dispatch on `element.type`. Lesson: when extending an Overpass query, check whether the new tag families use way/node/both before assuming the existing parser shape covers them.
- **`departureTime` defaults to `new Date()` so adding the param wasn't a breaking change.** Existing call sites in /home and /en-route still call `pickWinner(rawRoutes, allZones)` with no third argument — they implicitly mean "leave now," which is what most trips are. Forward-looking signature for when scheduling lands; zero churn today. Default values are an underrated tool for forward-compatible API additions.
- **Mock fallback grew with the categories.** Original mock was 3 polygons covering safe/caution/avoid. With the new categories shipping behind the same `SHOW_ZONES` debug toggle, the mock needed to demonstrate police/wildlife/road-condition zones too — otherwise the demo screenshot would be misleading when Overpass is unreachable. Six mock zones now, one per category. Mock fidelity is part of the demo experience, not a fallback afterthought.

---

## chore/figma-fidelity-audit-3 (2026-05-06)

Third fidelity audit, after three structural PRs (community-report, en-route, review-guidance). What we did, in plain terms:

- **Recurring miss across screens: tap-target compliance via hitSlop=8 instead of hitSlop=12.** Pattern showed up in /report (4 close-X / chevron-back buttons, 8pt → 12pt), making them HIG-compliant on the visual + slop math. Lesson: when copying the modal-header dismiss pattern, default to hitSlop=12 from the start. 8pt was the original value; 4pt nudge per side compounds across every modal we ship.
- **Daylight gradient had two competing palettes.** /home's bottom-sheet strip (orange→mauve→indigo, the literal colors of light from afternoon to night) and `lib/daylight.ts`'s polyline (green→yellow→orange→red, a severity scale) both encoded the same axis with different colors. The Figma's intent — confirmed via Route Experienced (825:3715) — is one canonical gradient: orange→mauve→indigo. The severity-style gradient ("good→bad") was inherited from a generic palette and contradicts the thesis register, which is "this is what your trip looks like at sunset," not "drive at risk." Updated `colorForMinutesToSunset` to sample the strip palette at 5 bands. One axis, one canonical encoding, one set of colors. Bottom-sheet strip and polyline now agree.
- **Welcome checkbox had no state.** It rendered as a Pressable with no `useState`, no `accessibilityState`, no toggled visual. VoiceOver couldn't tell it was checked; tap did nothing. Added a real boolean state, accessibility state, and a checkmark Ionicon that renders only when checked. Visual bumped 18→24pt with `hitSlop=20` for HIG compliance via the exception clause (genuinely-constrained dense row alongside legal copy). Unrelated original miss — first build was visual-only, missed the interaction half.
- **Search recent items: paddingVertical 4 → 10 was the cleanest HIG fix.** No hitSlop was set; the row was 32pt tall (icon 24 + 4×2). Bumping padding to 10pt brings total to 44pt — visual itself is HIG-compliant, no fallback math needed. Easier to reason about than hitSlop.
- **En-route utility buttons: the 32pt icon in 44pt frame was visually crowded** (only 6pt of pill visible per side). Dropped to 24pt icons; same 44pt frame now reads as "icon sitting inside pill" rather than "icon filling pill." The earlier iteration that bumped icons to 32pt was reaching for in-driving readability, but at the cost of the visual register. 24pt was the correct middle ground all along — Figma had it at 16pt, we found 24pt as the readable-without-crowding sweet spot.
- **Patterns ship faster than fixes.** Each of the five misses above was a single PR's worth of drift, but accumulated over three structural PRs they hit critical mass. Every audit, the pattern is the same: a few systemic misses (hitSlop, daylight gradient axis), a few one-offs (Welcome checkbox state). Auditing every ~5 PRs catches them before they become muscle memory.

---

## feat/review-guidance (2026-05-06)

The post-incident reflective Do/Have/Say/Know flow lands. What we did, in plain terms:

- **CLAUDE.md was wrong about /what-to-do being a tab navigator.** It described "/what-to-do as a tab navigator with four content variants." The real design — discovered by asking the user, not by re-reading docs — is a *sequential* flow: Officer/Trooper → Do → Have → Say → Know, traversed with chevrons, one screen at a time. Reflective post-incident review, not active-driving urgent guidance. The doc described the Figma's *content variants* and inferred the wrong navigation pattern. Lesson: when CLAUDE.md describes architecture, treat it as one source among many — confirm with the user *and* with Figma before building.
- **State machine in one route, not five separate routes.** Same play we used in /report (picker → detail → thank-you): one modal envelope, internal index 0–4, chevron back/forward decrements/increments. Sidesteps the iOS modal-on-modal stacking quirk this codebase has hit before (the prior /safety → /pulled-over → /armed-or-not chain). Cleaner state, cleaner architecture, faster build. Pattern reuse is its own win — every additional time we use it, the next person reaches for it sooner.
- **`router.dismissAll()` is the right exit for nested modal flows.** Plain `router.back()` from /review-guidance only pops one level, leaving the user on /armed-or-not (also a modal), which they'd have to dismiss. Then /safety. Three taps to escape after a stressful arc — bad UX. `dismissAll()` unwinds every stacked modal at once and lands on the underlying stack route (/en-route). Pattern worth remembering for any modal flow that's >1 deep.
- **Flow position determines the register, not the content.** The same Officer/Trooper screen we'd built for the active-pre-stop path turned out to belong in the *post*-stop reflective flow. The content didn't change; its position in the user journey did. Lesson: a screen's chrome and copy are determined by its job in the flow, not just by what's on it. Always confirm flow position before building.
- **Conditional rendering branched on a single param keeps the data shape flat.** The "What to Say" sub-view's first bullet (concealed-carry declaration) only renders when `armed=yes` or `preferred-not-to-answer`. One boolean (`showFirearm`), one if-block in the bullets array — no separate variants table, no duplicated screens. Simple branch in the view, single source of truth in the URL param.
- **Deleted /pulled-over.tsx in the same diff that absorbed its content.** Per anti-slop rule #5: "delete what you replace." Git history preserves the standalone Officer/Trooper screen if we ever want it again; the live tree stays uncluttered. No "in case we need it" leftovers.

---

## feat/en-route (2026-05-05)

The active-driving state. What we did, in plain terms:

- **The Figma metadata audit reframed the docket.** Before pulling the full Flow canvas, "What's NOT shipped" in CLAUDE.md called En-Route "out of scope." After listing the 38 designed screens, En-Route turned out to be the connective tissue between the technical claim (good routes) and the moral claim (handling stops): the safety modal, the report modal, the trip-summary popup all presuppose you're driving. The doc was wrong; pull the canvas before trusting the doc. Lesson: design docs decay, design files don't.
- **Map setup duplicated, not extracted (rule of three).** /home and /en-route share route fetching, zone fetching, scoring memos, useFocusEffect refresh, polyline rendering, circle rendering. About 80 lines of duplicated logic. The rule says inline twice, extract on three. Premature extraction would force a `<RoutedMap />` API decision before we know what /en-route's third sibling (Trip Summary? Two Zone Turn Card?) actually needs. Cheaper to copy-paste now and refactor when the shape is clear than to design for hypothetical future requirements.
- **`router.push` on /en-route gives free swipe-to-dismiss.** No explicit "End trip" button on the design — the system swipe-back gesture handles it because the screen is stack-pushed (not modal-presented). Trusting the platform affordance kept the chrome clean. Modal presentation would've broken the gesture; stack push respects it.
- **Side button column = the bridge.** Shield → /safety, Report → /report, Center → recenter. Three out of four buttons wire directly into existing flows. Building En-Route added zero new flows but unlocked four downstream ones (safety, report, recenter, future help). Highest leverage build of the session per line of code.
- **Placeholder turn instruction beats fake turn-by-turn.** OSRM gives route geometry, not turn-by-turn instructions. Faking instructions ("Turn left in 0.3 mi") at runtime would require either a routing engine or a hand-tuned heuristic that lies. Static placeholder ("Turn left onto South Cedar Street, 0.5 mi") communicates the design intent without claiming functionality we don't have. Same play as the photo-stub on /report — visible affordance, honest scope.
- **Title2/Emphasized was the missing token.** Turn instruction is 22/28/700/-0.26 — Title2/Emphasized in iOS HIG. Wasn't in `theme/typography.ts` because no prior screen needed it. Added it before using it (per anti-slop rule #2). Token system continues to grow only when the design demands it; not a token-completionist exercise.
- **iOS HIG > Figma — but with a real exception clause.** Mid-PR we promoted "44pt visual takes precedence over Figma" from suggestion to rule (`.cursorrules` + CLAUDE.md). Then immediately ran into the case the rule's exception clause was written for: 4 utility-secondary buttons in a dense status row, where 44pt visual would crowd the ETA number and read as primary actions. Resolved with 28pt visual + `hitSlop=12` (52pt effective tap area). The exception is "small icon inside a dense row where 44pt would break the layout" — and "break" includes soft visual break, not just literal overflow. Rule + exception clause encoded together so the next person reading the rule sees both halves.
- **Figma fidelity audit cadence is now codified.** Every ~5 PRs, branch `chore/figma-fidelity-audit-N`, diff every shipped screen against its Figma node, fix drift in one PR. Added to `docs/workflow.md` (step 12) and surfaced in `CLAUDE.md` (workflow step 9). Already 2 audits done informally — codifying made it part of the rhythm rather than something we'd notice was missing later. Five sub-44pt tap targets across the app (Welcome checkbox, pulled-over chevron + close, search recent items, report close X) noted but deferred to the next audit branch — keeps this PR scoped.

---

## feat/community-report (2026-05-05)

The full community-reporting flow lands. What we did, in plain terms:

- **Single screen, internal state machine — not three routes.** The Figma shows three "popups" (picker, detail, thank-you) but they all share one backdrop and one card. That's a state machine, not a router shape. `useState<'picker' | 'detail' | 'thank-you'>` + sub-component dispatch reads cleaner than three screens with inter-route param passing, AND avoids the iOS modal-stack quirk (see prior PR's note on /safety presentation). One screen, three views; the route boundary is the modal envelope, not the views.
- **`presentation: 'transparentModal'` is the right tool when you want a popup, not a sheet.** iOS's `'modal'` slides up from the bottom and takes over the screen; `'transparentModal'` fades in over the previous screen which stays mounted. The map underneath stays interactive for the duration. Use it for anything that's conceptually an overlay — drop-pin, action sheet, confirmation — not a navigated destination.
- **Refactored ranked-routes from `useState` to `useMemo`.** Old pattern: `setRoutes(pickWinner(...))` called from the fetch effect, twice (once with empty zones, once with full zones). New pattern: store `rawRoutes`, `osmZones`, `reportZones` as separate useState slots; derive `routes` via `useMemo`. Result: when reportZones updates from `useFocusEffect`, ranking recomputes automatically. No second effect needed. Three sources of truth, one derived value — cleaner than juggling setRoutes call sites.
- **`useFocusEffect` for "refresh on return-to-screen" patterns.** Submitting a report on /report and tapping Close should land the user back on /home with the new report already on the map. `useFocusEffect(useCallback(...))` runs on every focus, including initial mount and post-modal-dismissal. Cheaper than a polling interval, more reliable than imperative refresh calls. Signature requires `useCallback` to stabilize the effect's identity — copy-paste the pattern, don't forget the wrapper.
- **`Zone` discriminated union grew a new variant without breaking consumers.** Adding `'point'` to `'polygon' | 'polyline'` required: extending the type, adding a `case 'point'` branch in the scorer dispatch, and a new geometry-specific renderer. TypeScript caught the missing branch in scoring on the first compile. The reducer-style switch on `zone.geometry` is what makes the discriminated union earn its keep — adding a variant means adding one branch in each consumer, not auditing every callsite.
- **Anonymity at write-time, not at storage-time.** Sensitive-category reports never persist a `submittedBy` field. The user can't later "de-anonymize" a sensitive report because there's no record of who they were when they made it. Compare: storing the field but flagging it `private: true` — same UX, much more leakable. The strongest privacy guarantee is the absence of data.
- **Photo affordance as visible stub instead of hidden TODO.** The dashed-border tap target renders; tapping shows "coming soon." User sees the future of the feature without tripping over a half-built camera flow. Better than hiding it (loses context for designers/users) AND better than implementing a half-version (sets a ceiling on quality). Signal the intent; defer the work.
- **`addCommunityReport` returns the inserted report.** The Thank-You screen needs the id to support Undo. Returning the full report from `add(...)` is one extra line in the adapter and saves the consumer a separate `getById` call. Adapters that return what they wrote are friendlier than adapters that return void.

## feat/home-report-button (2026-05-05)

The community-reporting flow's entry point. What we did, in plain terms:

- **`onLayout` + conditional-render pattern for buttons anchored to a measured sibling.** The Report button floats 24pt above a bottom sheet whose height grows with content. State holds the measured height (init 0); the sheet's `onLayout` writes to it; the button only renders when `> 0`. One frame of "no button" is preferable to one frame of "button at the wrong position." Reusable shape for any future floating-anchored UI (e.g., the En-Route side-button column tracking the same sheet).
- **Expo-router unmatched routes interact weirdly with modal-presented routes in the stack.** Tapping a dead-link route then "back" landed on `/safety` (modal-presented) instead of `/home`, even after a fresh app start. Didn't dig into root cause — wrote a 15-line stub at `/report` instead. Sometimes a tool quirk is cheaper to paper over than to understand. The stub gets fully replaced by the next PR; the cost is low and the symptom is gone.
- **Scope creep can be the right call.** The handoff said "dead link to /report is fine for one PR." It wasn't, once the modal-stack quirk surfaced. Expanding PR scope by 15 lines to ship clean nav beats shipping a documented bug. Revisit the handoff's assumptions when reality disagrees with them.
- **Reserved-color rule grew without weakening.** Orange `alert-circle` for the Report identity icon could've been framed as an "exception to the rule." Instead it's framed as "consistent with the rule's intent" (reserved colors signal safety; reporting *is* a safety signal). Same outcome, but keeps the rule strong everywhere else — exceptions invite more exceptions; alignments don't.
- **The audit pays compounding interest.** Six Figma frames came back tightened after one round of feedback (tile gap 32→24, submit 36→44pt, anonymity disclosure promoted to title block, per-category CTA copy differentiated, Title1 Regular added). Auditing before coding once saved auditing after coding three times.

---

## chore/figma-fidelity-audit-2 (2026-05-05)

Second audit pass on the lighter screens. What we did, in plain terms:

- **Recurring miss across screens: M3 Elevation 1 shadow on filled buttons.** Welcome (Get started + Log in), Onboarding (Continue), Permissions (Settings) all specced an Elevation 1 shadow in Figma — a small drop shadow that lifts the pill button off the surface — that I'd missed during initial builds. Added the same approximation pattern: `shadowOffset: {0, 1}, shadowOpacity: 0.15, shadowRadius: 3, elevation: 2`. Outlined buttons (Get Started's auth options, Permissions sub-rows) correctly don't have shadows.
- **Get Started: replaced `spacerTop: 200` hack with flex centering.** Original used a 200pt View to push title down; restructured to use `flex: 1, alignItems: 'center', justifyContent: 'center'` on outer + `width: 326, gap: 88` on inner wrapper. Same visual on iPhone 14, but now responsive across device heights — content centers on whatever screen.
- **Welcome: replaced `marginTop: 16` on subtitle with `gap: 16` on titleBlock.** Same visual outcome, but matches Figma's flex column structure rather than CSS-style margins. Cleaner hierarchy.
- **Spot-checks passed for Home and Safety modal.** Home's bottom sheet was rebuilt against Figma 825:3635 carefully; Safety modal got fixed yesterday + the user consolidated the footer to TrustedContactStatus. Nothing additional to fix.
- **Audit pattern that's worked across both passes.** Pull Figma node → scan for load-bearing structural patterns (flex hierarchies, gap values, justify-*, fixed dimensions, shadows) → compare structurally → fix. Don't chase pixel-level deltas; the structure is what reads as "right" or "off." Most screens needed only minor tweaks once the pattern was established.

---

## chore/figma-fidelity-audit (2026-05-04)

Audit pass on the screens flagged for spacing/structural fidelity issues. What we did, in plain terms:

- **Pulled-over: same flex-1 + justify-end miss as armed-or-not.** Close link was sitting `gap: 40` below the cards instead of being pushed to the bottom of available space. Fixed by adding a `closeArea` wrapper with `flex: 1, justifyContent: 'flex-end'` — same pattern that fixed armed-or-not.
- **Search: full Landing rebuild.** Previous version was a barebones text input with back chevron — actual Figma shows gray-fill search bar (chevron + input + mic), Quick Tools horizontal scroll (Saved/Trending/Food/Gas/Parking), Fuel section CTA, Recent searches list. Rebuilt to match.
- **Hardcoded widths fail on wider iPhones.** `width: 374` was set on the search bar (and `width: 358` on the menu row) on a 390pt iPhone 14 baseline. On iPhone 14 Pro Max (430pt) or 16 Pro Max (440pt), `width: 374` centered creates a 28pt+ edge margin instead of the intended 8pt. **Fix: `alignSelf: 'stretch'` + `marginHorizontal: 8`** — preserves the "8pt from each edge" design intent across all device widths. Applied to both /search and components/SearchBar.tsx; menuRow got the analogous `marginHorizontal: 16` fix.
- **Audit methodology that worked.** Pull the Figma node via MCP, scan its source for the load-bearing structural patterns (flex-1, gap values, justify-content, items-*, fixed dimensions). Compare to my implementation. Look for missing wrappers, missing flex-1 + justify-* combinations, hardcoded widths that don't scale. Fix the structural misses; ignore pixel-level minor differences.
- **Audit lesson worth keeping.** "Lands but feels off" feedback is signal that a structural pattern was missed (typically flex-1 + justify-* for "fill remaining space"), not that individual values are wrong. Match Figma's hierarchy, not just its numbers.
